Excavation of the Han Tombs at Guangzhou

(Abstract)

In the surburbs of Guangzhou, in preparing grounds for a suburban development project of the municipal government, 409 tombs of the Han Dynasty were excavated from 1953 to 1960. This report is divided into eight chapters. Chapter One is an introduction, Chapter Seven is devoted to the study of the chronology of these tombs and Chapter Eight is a conclusion of the whole work. Chapters Two to Six classify the tombs according to their chronological order. They fall into five periods: the early Western Han Dynasty, the middle Western Han Dynasty, the late Western Han Dynasty, the early Eastern Han Dynasty and the late Eastern Han Dynasty. Each period takes up one chapter. However, the classification of the form of the tombs and funerary objects is according to a scheme applicable to the whole.

Of the 409 tombs excavated, there are 29 shaft tombs, 291 tombs of wooden chambers and 89 tombs of brick chambers. According to their different outward forms and internal structures, they are further differentiated into 22 types of seven categories.

There are 182 tombs of the early Western Han Dynasty, 21 of which are shaft tombs. They are small tombs and their funerary objects are simple and few in number. All the rest are tombs of wooden chambers, ranging from small to medium in size. Some of these tombs have "waist-pits" or are paved with gravels. This is rather peculiar. The owners of these tombs were probably Nanyue people (minority nationality formerly living in modern Guangdong and Guangxi). Though the burial of more than one person in one tomb had begun at this time, in most of these tombs only one person was found interred. The grouping of the pottery ware in each tomb took one of the following three combinations:

(1) "weng" (jar) and "guan" (pot),
(2) vessels of local origin, featuring local colour: "pou" (small wine vessel), "shuangerguan" (double-handled pot), tripod pot (including all kinds of joined pots), small box and tripod box, and
(3) vessels of the culture of the Central Plain: ding-tripod pot, jar and square jar.

According to these three combinations of pottery vessels, the 182 tombs may be divided into two groups. Group One contains only the vessels in Combination (1) or those in Combinations (1) and (2). There are 54 tombs in this group. Their owners were probably Nanyue people. Group Two contains either only the vessels in Combination (3) or all those in the three combinations. There are 125 tombs in this group. Their owners were mainly Han people.

A considerable number of bronze and iron weapons were found in these tombs. Some bronze ware such as mirrors, swords, halberds and spears, and some lacquer ware such as plates, caskets and boxes found are like those unearthed in tombs at Changsha of the Warring States and the early Western Han Dynasty. The seals found in some of the medium and large tombs show that their owners were the nobles of the Nanyue Kingdom. The nobles also had their own cemetery.

There are 64 tombs of the middle Western Han Dynasty. They resemble the tombs of the previous period in form and construction, but there are fewer large ones. Some of the tomb chambers have two storeys and some two rooms, one back, one front. They were obviously modelled on the dwellings of the living. By this time the practice of successive interments had become quite common.

A comparison of the pottery of this period with that of the previous one shows three changes. First, there were more glazed ones, and the craftsmanship reached a higher level but the decoration showed a tendency for the simple and primitive. Secondly, all the pottery forms of local colour such as the pou, tripod pot, small box and tripod box had almost completely disappeared. This shows that the two cultures of the Han and Yue and their ethnical traits had thoroughly merged. Thirdly, pottery models of such objects as well, stoves, round and square granary, and dwelling were now more common. All the dwellings shown by the pottery models were of the pile-structure type. The dwelling built on the raised platform of this structure was for the housing of man and the shed beneath the platform was used for raising and keeping domestic animals and fowls. A strange phenomenon of this period is the sharp fall in the number of iron weapons in comparison with the previous one. The two wood models of boat, the personal ornaments of beads and the pottery lamp held by human figure unearthed are concrete evidences of the increased trade relations with the South China Sea countries during the reign of Emperor Wu Di of the Han Dynasty.

There are 32 tombs of the late Western Han Dynasty. The structure of the tomb was still of the shaft type with wood chamber for the coffin, but there were already tombs with a construction plan resembling that of the large brick tombs of the Eastern Han Dynasty. There was a wider variety of the pottery form of this period and models of dwelling, granary and stove were now even more common than before. Pottery models show that the pile-structure dwelling now was no longer rectangular. It became L-shaped with the addition of a structure to the rear of one end of it. The new addition was used as a lavatory. This separation of the toilet from the main dwelling represented a great advancement in the standard of living of the time. There was an increase in the pottery models of animals. All these reflected indirectly the development of agriculture of the region. There were more bronze mirrors, copper coins buried with the dead, but iron weapons became even more scarce. Personal ornaments of beads were found in two-thirds of the tombs and there was an increase in the number of pottery lamps held by human figures. These finds show that the overseas trade was quite prosperous during this period.

There are 41 tombs of the early Eastern Han Dynasty. Besides shaft tombs with wooden coffin chambers tombs with brick chambers had appeared. The pottery vessels are similar to those of the late Western Han Dynasty, but there are some changes in their forms. There are more varieties of pottery models of dwellings, but the pile-structure one has disappeared. There are instead models of L-shaped bungalows and storeyed high buildings. This shows that at the time of these tombs there had been important development in architecture. The practice of burying more than one person in a single tomb had become wide spread. The pottery models of painted multi-decked boats and the large number of personal ornaments of beads unearthed were without doubt closely connected with the development of foreign communications and the art of shipbuilding during this period.

There are 90 tombs of the late Eastern Han Dynasty. There were fewer wood chamber tombs, but brick chamber tombs had become very popular. Some large ones of the latter had many chambers. This may mean that they were built for the burial of many persons. Bronze mirrors were found in most tombs. There were pottery models of objects that had not appeared before. They were courtyards with bungalows built on three sides, heavily-defended fortresses, corpulent pottery figures bearing lamp and model boats with complete structural details.

The time occupied by the above tombs was divided into five periods.

The first period began from the 28th year (219 B.C.) of Emperor Qin Shihuang and lasted till somewhere between the reigns of Emperor Wen Di and Emperor Jing Di of the Western Han Dynasty. This was the early period of the Kingdom of Nanyue.

The second period began from the end of the first period and lasted till 111 B.C., the 6th year of the Yuan Ding reign of Emperor Wu Di when the Kingdom of Nanyue was destroyed. This was the late period of the Kingdom of Nanyue.

The third period followed the second. i.e. 111 B.C. and ended in between the reigns of Emperor Yuan Di and Emperor Cheng Di, a period of about 80 years. This was the middle Western Han Dynasty.

The fourth period succeeded the third one and ended with the fall of the New Dynasty of the usurper Wang Mang, a period of about of 50 years. This was the late Western Han Dynasty.

The fifth period began from the first year of the Jian Wu reign and ended immediately before the beginning of the Jian Chu reign (A.D. 76), again a period of about 50 years. This was the early Eastern Han Dynasty.

Four of the tombs of the late Eastern Han Dynasty had tomb stones with inscriptions recording the dates of each of them.

(1) A.D. 76 for Tomb No. 5041,

(2) A.D. 80 for Tomb No. 5060,

(3) A.D. 97 for Tomb No. 5065, and

(4) A.D. 170 for Tomb No. 5068.

The dates of these four tombs are important criterions for dating the tombs of this period. The earliest of these tombs was built in the first year of the Jian Chu reign and the latest in the last year of the Eastern Han Dynasty. In between them stretched a period of about 140 years (A.D. 76-220).

These Han Dynasty tombs at Guangzhou were mainly located on the hills on the outskirts of the city, though they were also discovered within the city proper. Tombs have been unearthed about one kilometer east and west from the centre of the city. The closeness of the sites of these tombs from the centre of the city proper shows that Guangzhou was much smaller in the early Han Dynasty than it was in the later period. After the middle Western Han Dynasty, as the city grew in area, tombs were built farther and farther from its centre. Thus, the locations of tombs at different times can be used as a basis for the study of the history of the city's development.

The Nanyue people are a branch of the ancient Baiyue (Hundred Yues) people. They were the aborigines of ancient Guangzhou. The tombs of the early Western Han Dynasty were built during the period of the Nanyue Kingdom, that was established between the Qin and Han Dynasties. It was a period when the authority of the Han Dynasty court was completely ignored and the former prefectures (a local administration) now acted just like independent sovereigns. This was what the Nanyue Kingdom did. The knowledge we derived from these tombs tells us that during the early Western Han Dynasty, the Han people and the Yue people lived together amicably without any discrimination. The two cultures existed side by side. When Emperor Wu Di of the Western Han Dynasty reconquered Lingnan (present Guangdong Province), the merger and mutual assimilation of the two cultures was further accelerated. This was collaborated by the contents of the tombs of the middle Western Han Dynasty.

From the iron tools, iron farm implements, cereals and seeds and kernels of various fruits, from the fine craftsmanship of the lacquer ware, the earthen ware and the carpentry, from the inscriptions on the pottery vessels and from the fact that only the banliang coins and no other coins were discovered in the tombs of the early Western Han Dynasty, it can be seen clearly that large numbers of the Han people had migrated into Lingnan from the Central Plain at this time. And they went with their cattle-drawn plows, iron farm implements and iron tools together with the advanced culture of the Han people. This gave a great impetus to the political, economic and cultural development of the region. With Guangzhou as its centre, the Lingnan region quickly caught up with the advance of the history of the whole country. However, after the middle Western Han Dynasty, there was a sudden sharp decline in

the number of iron weapons. This was directly contradictory to the situation in the Central Plain and the other parts of the country at that time. This might have been due to the absence of iron mining and metallurgical industries in the region.

Guangzhou lies on the shore of the South China Sea. The local weather is hot and damp with plenty of rain. The pottery models of the various types of dwellings, round and square granaries show that all that for man and cereals were built above the ground and with windows or holes for ventilation. This was conditioned by the local climate and it became a characteristics of the Han Dynasty buildings of the region. Guangzhou was geographically situated as the hub of land and water communications of the whole region. Rivers from all directions converge on it and they are the natural basis for the development of water communications. The development of the art of shipbuilding and navigation was reflected in the wood and pottery models of various kinds of ships and boats unearthed from these Guangzhou Han tombs. The presence of beads for personal ornaments in more tombs is another proof of such development. Censers were also found in most tombs. While incense wood was imported mainly from Southeast Asia, some beads made of various materials were imported from overseas. Especially worth notice are the pottery models of well-defended fortresses and lamp held by human figures with deep-set eyes and big nose. The fortresses were reflections of sharp class confliction and the rise of local powers during the Eastern Han Dynasty. The deep-eyed and big-nosed figures are concrete evidence of the commercial relations between Guangzhou and the South Sea countries. Such tomb figurines first appeared in tombs of the middle Western Han Dynasty and became more numerous afterwards. They were only occasionally found in Eastern Han Dynasty tombs excavated in County Guexian, Guangxi Province and at Changsha, Hunan Province. They were never found elsewhere. They have short head, broad noses, broad cheek-bones, thick lips and protruded chin and are of medium height. Their beard and chest hair is well developed. All these features make them appear like the aborigines of Indonesia — “proto-Malayans”. They have their heads turbaned and they wear bun of coiled hair. The servant figures wear long skirts. They are all naked to the waist. They are dressed like the natives of islands of Indonesia. According to our preliminary study of them, they are probably the images of the Kunlun slaves (negroes) we find in Chinese historical documents. About the second centruy B.C., Indonesia was in the first stage of slavery society and some of the slaves were sold abroad as commodities. During the Han Dynasty, the chief port in the South China Sea visited by Chinese traders was Huangzhi(黄支) (probably Kancipura, modern Conjeneram) on the southern coast of India. However, Chinese vessels, on their way to the Indian port, passed through Pizong (皮宗), probably an Indonesian port. These negroes might have been bought either at this port or transported by Indian slave traders. In short, they were imported slaves and sold at Guangzhou or nearby to the rich, aristocratic and influential families as “domestic servants”. Their images as tomb figurines were buried with their masters so that they might continue to serve their masters in the nether world.
